{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 136.66 -0.44 L 224.14 -0.44}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{162.18527pt}{-0.32pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{110.2081pt}{3.78276pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\right)\ ,

(6.82)

with the term containing
TM
now in degree zero.

Since the last differential here is surjective, the canonical morphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{0}({\check{C}}_{0}\,\pazocal{U})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{0}({\check{C}}_{0}\,\pazocal{U})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{0}({\check{C}}_{n-2}\,\pazocal{U})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{0}({\check{C}}_{n-2}\,\pazocal{U})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\big\{(X,f)\in TM\oplus\Omega^{0}({\check{C}}_{n-1}\,\pazocal{U})\,\big|\,\mathrm{d}\log g(X)-\check{\delta}f=0\big\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM\oplus\Omega^{0}({\check{C}}_{n-1}\,\pazocal{U})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker(\check{\delta})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-1)^{n-1}{\mathrm{id}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-1)^{n-1}\,\check{\delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{\delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-1)^{3}\,\check{\delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{\delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-1)^{2}\,\check{\delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{id}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{\delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}g(-)+\check{\delta}}

(6.83)

where the signs of the horizontal morphisms
{\mathrm{id}}
alternate, is a weak equivalence from the dgla
{\check{C}}{\mathrm{At}}(g)
; this morphism respects the brackets because only brackets with elements in degree zero are non-trivial.
That is, there is a canonical equivalence

{\mathfrak{sym}}(g)\simeq{\check{C}}{\mathrm{At}}(g)

(6.84)

of dglas.

\triangleleft

Forgetting the respective highest form degrees of the connection data provides a chain of moduli stacks

\underline{{\mathscr{G}\mathrm{rb}}}^{n-1}_{\nabla}(M)_{v}\longrightarrow\underline{{\mathscr{G}\mathrm{rb}}}^{n-1}_{\nabla|n-1}(M)_{v}\longrightarrow\underline{{\mathscr{G}\mathrm{rb}}}^{n-1}_{\nabla|n-2}(M)_{v}\longrightarrow\cdots\longrightarrow\underline{{\mathscr{G}\mathrm{rb}}}^{n-1}_{\nabla|1}(M)_{v}\longrightarrow\underline{{\mathscr{G}\mathrm{rb}}}^{n-1}(M)_{v}\ .

(6.85)

These forgetful maps are compatible with the abelian group structure and
\mathsf{Diff}(M)
actions, and so we obtain an associated chain of dglas

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{sym}}(g,\pazocal{A})\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{sym}}^{\leqslant n-1}(g,\pazocal{A})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{sym}}^{\leqslant n-2}(g,\pazocal{A})\longrightarrow\cdots\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{sym}}^{\leqslant 1}(g,\pazocal{A})\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{sym}}(g)\ ,

(6.86)

where
[g,\pazocal{A}]
is an
(n{-}1)
-gerbe with full connection,
{\mathfrak{sym}}^{\leqslant p}(g,\pazocal{A}):={\mathfrak{sym}}(g,{\mathrm{fgt}}_{p+1,\dots,n}\,\pazocal{A})
and
{\mathrm{fgt}}_{p+1,\ldots,n}
is the morphism which forgets form degrees
n,n-1,\ldots,p+1
of the connection data, for
0\leqslant p\leqslant n-1
.

By combining Theorem
5.27
together with Remark
6.19
, Theorem
6.54
and Remark
6.80
, we are then led to formulate

Conjecture 6.87

Let
H\in\Omega_{\rm cl}^{n+1}(M)
be the field strength of the
(n{-}1)
-gerbe connection
\pazocal{A}
.
There is a canonical equivalence

\mathrm{Cour}_{n-1}(H)\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }{\mathfrak{sym}}^{\leqslant n-1}(g,\pazocal{A})

(6.88)

of
L_{\infty}
-algebras over
TM
.

Conjecture
6.87
is supported by Example
6.99
below, which shows that the conjecture is true for
n=2
. It also agrees with the general expectations sketched in
[
38
, Example 3.2.8]
.

Remark 6.89

From the discussion preceding Theorem
6.54
, it is tempting to further conjecture an analogous equivalence between the
L_{\infty}
-algebras
\mathrm{Cour}_{n-1}^{\leqslant p}(H)
of Proposition
6.45
and
{\mathfrak{sym}}^{\leqslant p}(g,\pazocal{A})
for all
0\leqslant p\leqslant n-1
. For
p=0
the conjecture is true by Lemma
6.52
. This would identify
\mathrm{Cour}_{n-1}^{\leqslant p}(H)
as the
L_{\infty}
-algebra controlling the infinitesimal symmetries of an
(n{-}1)
-gerbe with
p
-form connection, for each
0\leqslant p\leqslant n-1
. However, in general we lack a geometric interpretation of
\mathrm{Cour}_{n-1}^{\leqslant p}(H)
as an extension of some algebroid structure arising from the data of an
(n{-}1)
-gerbe with
p
-form connection, so there is currently no basis for such an expectation.

\triangleleft

Conjecture 6.90

There is a commutative diagram of
L_{\infty}
-algebras over
TM

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Cour}_{n-1}(H)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathfrak{sym}}^{\leqslant n-1}(g,\pazocal{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{At}}_{n-1}(H)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\check{C}}{\mathrm{At}}(g)}

(6.91)

where the top horizontal arrow is the weak equivalence of Conjecture
6.87
, the bottom horizontal arrow is induced by the
\infty
-equivalence from Theorem
5.27
, the left vertical arrow is the canonical
\infty
-projection of Theorem
6.54
, and the right vertical arrow is induced from the composition of the weak equivalence from Remark
6.80
with the chain of morphisms in (
6.86
).

Remark 6.92

For
n=2
, the statement of Conjecture
6.90
is similar to that of
[
37
, Proposition 5.2.6]
. Conjectures
6.87
and
6.90
are evidently true for
n=1
by Example
6.38
.

\triangleleft

Example 6.93

Let
[g,A]
be a
{\mathrm{U}}(1)
-bundle with connection on
M
.
Its associated dgla reads as

{\mathfrak{sym}}(g,A)=\left(\hbox to369.56pt{\vbox to20.56pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 184.7779pt\lower-9.65973pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-184.7779pt}{-3.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -135.02 -0.69 L -106.6 -0.69}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-76.84184pt}{-0.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-93.32005pt}{3.88608pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\right)

(6.94)

where the first term sits in degree zero.
Explicitly, the differential reads as

\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{pb}}(X,\omega^{0}_{0})=(-\check{\delta}\omega^{0}_{0}+\mathrm{d}\log g(X),\,-\mathrm{d}\omega^{0}_{0}+\pounds_{X}A)\ ,

(6.95)

where the minus signs stem from the shift of
E
.

Note that
{\mathfrak{sym}}(g,A)
is not an
L_{\infty}
-algebroid over
M
, but it is an
L_{\infty}
-algebra over the Lie algebra
\Gamma(M,TM)
(it lives purely on the right-hand side of the square in Theorem
3.14
).
Consequently, we cannot ask for
p
-form connections on
[g,A]
in the sense of Definition
4.21
, but—inspired by that definition—we can still ask for
C^{\infty}(M)
-linear 1-splittings of the canonical projection
{\mathfrak{sym}}(g,A)\longrightarrow TM
.
These splittings could still be interpreted as 1-form connections on the bundle with 1-form connection
[g,A]
in some weak sense.

Such a 1-splitting consists of a
C^{\infty}(M)
-linear map
TM\longrightarrow TM\oplus\Omega^{0}({\check{C}}_{0}\,\pazocal{U})
which is the identity on
TM
; that is, it is given by a locally defined 1-form
a\in\Omega^{1}({\check{C}}_{0}\,\pazocal{U})
.
This map has to satisfy a Maurer–Cartan condition, which in this case unravels to the condition that

\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{pb}}\circ({\mathrm{id}}_{TM},a)=0\ .

(6.96)

Explicitly, for
X\in TM
we compute

\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{pb}}\circ({\mathrm{id}}_{TM},a)(X)=\big(-\check{\delta}a(X)+\mathrm{d}\log g(X),\,-\mathrm{d}\,\iota_{X}a+\pounds_{X}A\big)\ .

(6.97)

Vanishing of the first component is equivalent to
a\in\Omega^{1}({\check{C}}_{0}\,\pazocal{U})
defining a connection on the bundle
[g]
.
For the second component, we can thus write
a=A+\check{\delta}\alpha
, where
\alpha\in\Omega^{1}(M)
is a globally defined 1-form on
M
.
It then remains to solve the equation

0=-\mathrm{d}\,\iota_{X}(A+\alpha)+\pounds_{X}A\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad\iota_{X}\,\mathrm{d}A-\mathrm{d}\,\iota_{X}\alpha=0\ ,

(6.98)

for all vector fields
X
.
Since the first term is
C^{\infty}(M)
-linear in
X
, whereas the second term is not, the only way to solve the equation is with
\mathrm{d}A=0
and
\alpha=0
.
It follows that
{\mathfrak{sym}}(g,A)
admits a
C^{\infty}(M)
-linear 1-splitting only if its connection
A
is flat, and in that case the only such splitting is given by
A
itself.

Heuristically, this can be understood as stating that a bundle with a 1-form connection, seen as a geometric object, admits a 1-form connection
compatible with its existing 1-form connection only if the latter is flat.
Moreover, even then the only admissible additional 1-form connection coincides with the existing one (which is compatible with itself precisely because it is flat).

\triangleleft

Example 6.99

We now consider a 1-gerbe with 1-form connection
[g,A^{{(1)}}]\in{\mathscr{G}\mathrm{rb}}^{1}_{\nabla|1}(M)
; a 1-form connection on a 1-gerbe is more commonly called a
connective structure
.
By Proposition
6.61
and (
6.77
) we find that

{\mathfrak{sym}}(g,A^{{(1)}})=\left(\hbox to289.5pt{\vbox to20.06pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 144.74818pt\lower-8.97974pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-144.74818pt}{-2.82pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-82.2066pt}{4.06607pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{79.15091pt}{4.06607pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\right)\ ,

(6.100)

with the first term in degree
-1
.
Its bracket is given by the Lie action of vector fields on functions and differential forms.

We claim that this dgla is equivalent to its sub-dgla
{\mathfrak{sym}}^{\prime}(g,A^{{(1)}})
with underlying cochain complex

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{0}({\check{C}}_{0}\,\pazocal{U})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker(\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{pb}})\ ,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{pb}}}

(6.101)

where explicitly

\displaystyle\ker(\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{pb}})=\big\{

\displaystyle(X,\omega^{0}_{1},\omega^{1}_{0})\in TM\oplus\Omega^{0}({\check{C}}_{1}\,\pazocal{U})\oplus\Omega^{1}({\check{C}}_{0}\,\pazocal{U})\,\big|

(6.102)

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt-\check{\delta}\omega^{0}_{1}+\mathrm{d}\log g(X)=0\ ,\,-\mathrm{d}\omega^{0}_{1}+\check{\delta}\omega^{1}_{0}+\pounds_{X}A^{(1)}=0\big\}\ .

(6.103)

This amounts to showing that the map
TM\oplus\Omega^{0}({\check{C}}_{1}\,\pazocal{U})\oplus\Omega^{1}({\check{C}}_{0}\,\pazocal{U})\longrightarrow\ker(\mathrm{D})
is surjective.

To that end, recall that an element in
\ker(\mathrm{D})
is a pair

(\omega^{0}_{2},\omega^{1}_{1})\ \in\ \Omega^{0}({\check{C}}_{2}\,\pazocal{U})\oplus\Omega^{1}({\check{C}}_{1}\,\pazocal{U})

(6.104)

such that

\check{\delta}\omega^{0}_{2}=0\qquad,\qquad-\mathrm{d}\omega^{0}_{2}+\check{\delta}\omega^{1}_{1}=0\ .

(6.105)

Since
\check{\delta}\omega^{0}_{2}=0
, there exists
\mu^{0}_{1}\in\Omega^{0}({\check{C}}_{1}\,\pazocal{U})
such that
\omega^{0}_{2}=\check{\delta}\mu^{0}_{1}
.
In particular, each pair
(\omega^{0}_{2},\omega^{1}_{1})
is cohomologous to a pair of the form
(0,\omega^{1}_{1})
(for some new
\omega^{1}_{1}
) via the image of the element
(0,\mu^{0}_{1},0)\in TM\oplus\Omega^{0}({\check{C}}_{1}\,\pazocal{U})\oplus\Omega^{1}({\check{C}}_{0}\,\pazocal{U})
under
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{pb}}
.
An element
(0,\omega^{1}_{1})\in\ker(\mathrm{D})
now satisfies
\check{\delta}\omega^{1}_{1}=0
, so that we find
\mu^{1}_{0}\in\Omega^{1}({\check{C}}_{0}\,\pazocal{U})
with
\check{\delta}\mu^{1}_{0}=\omega^{1}_{1}
.
The residual equivalence relation remaining after restricting to the subspace of
\ker(\mathrm{D})
consisting of pairs
(0,\omega^{1}_{1})
is generated by the elements of
TM\oplus\Omega^{0}({\check{C}}_{1}\,\pazocal{U})\oplus\Omega^{1}({\check{C}}_{0}\,\pazocal{U})
of the form
(X,\omega^{0}_{1},\omega^{1}_{0})
with
-\check{\delta}\omega^{0}_{1}+\mathrm{d}\log g(X)=0
.
In particular, the element
(0,0,\mu^{1}_{0})
establishes the identity
[0,\omega^{1}_{1}]=0
in cohomology.

This concludes the argument, showing that the canonical morphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{0}({\check{C}}_{0}\,\pazocal{U})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{0}({\check{C}}_{0}\,\pazocal{U})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker(\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{pb}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM\oplus\Omega^{0}({\check{C}}_{1}\,\pazocal{U})\oplus\Omega^{1}({\check{C}}_{0}\,\pazocal{U})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker(\mathrm{D})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{id}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{pb}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{pb}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{pb}}}

(6.106)

is a quasi-isomorphism of dglas.

The dgla
{\mathfrak{sym}}^{\prime}(g,A^{{(1)}})
coincides with the dgla constructed by Collier from a gerbe with connective structure in
[
28
, Definition 10.38 and Proposition 10.48]
.
Furthermore, by
[
28
, Theorem 12.50]
this 2-term dgla is canonically equivalent to the 2-term
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathrm{Cour}_{1}(H)
of sections of the Courant algebroid associated to
(g,A^{{(1)}})
via the generalised tangent bundle construction of Remark
6.19
.

Gerbes with 1-form connection on
M
are classified by maps
[g,A^{{(1)}}]\colon M\longrightarrow{\mathrm{B}}\,{\mathrm{B}}_{\nabla}{\mathrm{U}}(1)
.
Since
{\mathrm{B}}_{\nabla}{\mathrm{U}}(1)
is an abelian group object, these classifying maps are of the form
x\colon M\longrightarrow{\mathrm{B}}G
for some group object
G
, and we can thus understand a 1-gerbe with connective structure on
M
as a
{\mathrm{B}}_{\nabla}{\mathrm{U}}(1)
-principal
\infty
-bundle on
M
.
Note, however, that
{\mathrm{B}}_{\nabla}{\mathrm{U}}(1)
does not have deformation theory (see Example
2.95
), and so there are no connections on
[g,A^{{(1)}}]
in the sense of Definition
4.21
.
There may, however, be connections in a weaker sense:
as in Example
6.93
, we conclude this example by investigating the
C^{\infty}(M)
-linear 1-splittings of the projection
{\mathfrak{sym}}(g,A^{(1)})\longrightarrow TM
of
L_{\infty}
-algebras (rather than of
L_{\infty}
-algebroids).

Such a 1-splitting is a
C^{\infty}(M)
-linear map

({\mathrm{id}}_{TM},a,b)\colon TM\longrightarrow TM\oplus\Omega^{0}({\check{C}}_{1}\,\pazocal{U})\oplus\Omega^{1}({\check{C}}_{0}\,\pazocal{U})

(6.107)

over
TM
satisfying the Maurer–Cartan condition, which in this case reads as

\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{pb}}\circ({\mathrm{id}}_{TM},a,b)=0\ .

(6.108)

Here
a\in\Omega^{1}({\check{C}}_{1}\,\pazocal{U})
and
b\in\Omega^{1}({\check{C}}_{0}\,\pazocal{U},T\,\pazocal{U})
, or equivalently
b
is a family of locally defined rank 2 tensors.
Note that, in general,
b
has no symmetry properties (it is neither symmetric nor antisymmetric as a map
T^{\vee}{\check{C}}_{0}\,\pazocal{U}\otimes T^{\vee}{\check{C}}_{0}\,\pazocal{U}\longrightarrow C^{\infty}({\check{C}}_{0}\,\pazocal{U})
).
Explicitly, given a vector field
X\in TM
, the condition
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{pb}}\circ({\mathrm{id}}_{TM},a,b)=0
amounts to

-\check{\delta}a(X)+\mathrm{d}\log(g)(X)=0\qquad,\qquad\check{\delta}\big(b(X)\big)-\mathrm{d}\big(a(X)\big)+\pounds_{X}A^{(1)}=0\ .

(6.109)

The first equation states that
a
is a connective structure on the gerbe defined by the
{\mathrm{U}}(1)
-cocycle
g
.
We can thus write
a=A^{(1)}+\check{\delta}\alpha
, for some
\alpha\in\Omega^{1}({\check{C}}_{0}\,\pazocal{U})
.
The second equation then becomes

\check{\delta}\big(b(X)\big)+\mathrm{d}A^{{(1)}}(X)-\mathrm{d}\,\iota_{X}\alpha=0\ ,

(6.110)

for all vector fields
X
.
Since the first two terms are
C^{\infty}(M)
-linear in
X
, but the third is not, the only way to satisfy this identity for all
X\in TM
simultaneously is by demanding that

\check{\delta}\big(b(X)\big)+\mathrm{d}A^{{(1)}}(X)=0\qquad,\qquad\mathrm{d}\,\iota_{X}\alpha=0\ .

(6.111)

Here the second identity states that
\iota_{X}\alpha=\alpha(X)
is locally constant, for each
X\in TM
.
Since
\alpha
is
C^{\infty}(M)
-linear, the only solution is
\alpha=0
.
That is, the connective structure
a
necessarily coincides with the connective structure
A^{{(1)}}
.
Finally, the first identity is equivalent to saying that
b
is an
enhanced curving
on
(g,A^{{(1)}})
in the sense of
[
86
, Definition 3.3]
.

This provides a new and generalised perspective on enhanced curvings.
A particularly interesting point is that, if we split the 2-tensor
b=A^{{(2)}}+\gamma
into its antisymmetric and symmetric parts, then
A^{{(2)}}
is a classical curving on
(g,A^{{(1)}})
, whilst the symmetric part
\gamma
satisfies
\check{\delta}\gamma=0
and thus defines a global symmetric 2-tensor field on
M
.
If we impose the additional condition on
b
that its symmetric part
\gamma
be positive definite, then the 1-splitting—or equivalently enhanced curving—
b
encodes simultaneously the
B
-field and Riemannian metric of, for instance, NSNS supergravity.
This was one of the motivations for introducing enhanced curvings in
[
86
]
.

\triangleleft

Recalling the discussion of Remark
4.35
, Example
6.99
as well as results of
[
86
]
motivate the following conjecture:
it is known
[
86
,
91
,
92
]
that the extension

{\mathrm{B}}^{2}{\mathrm{U}}(1)\longrightarrow{\mathrm{String}}(K)\longrightarrow K

has an enhancement to an extension

{\mathrm{B}}\,{\mathrm{B}}_{\nabla}{\mathrm{U}}(1)\longrightarrow{\mathrm{String}}_{\rm CS}(K)\longrightarrow K\ ,

where
{\mathrm{B}}_{\nabla}{\mathrm{U}}(1)
is the classifying stack for
{\mathrm{U}}(1)
-bundles with connection.
A classifying map

P_{K}:M\longrightarrow{\mathrm{B}}K

thus induces a gerbe
with connective structure
on the principal
K
-bundle
P_{K}
, which is the obstruction to a geometric string structure.

Conjecture 6.112

Let
{\operatorname{Hom}}_{v}(M,{\mathrm{B}}^{2}\,{\mathrm{B}}_{\nabla}{\mathrm{U}}(1))
and
{\operatorname{Hom}}_{v}(M,{\mathrm{B}}{\mathrm{String}}_{\rm CS}(K))
denote the full substacks of the respective internal homs where all the 1-forms induced via pullback by the connective structure of the multiplicative gerbe on
K
are vertical, i.e. they only have form legs along
M
(compare Example
2.92
).
Then:

(1)

The stacks
{\operatorname{Hom}}_{v}(M,{\mathrm{B}}^{2}\,{\mathrm{B}}_{\nabla}{\mathrm{U}}(1))/\mathsf{Diff}(M)
and
{\operatorname{Hom}}_{v}(M,{\mathrm{B}}{\mathrm{String}}_{\rm CS}(K))/\mathsf{Diff}(M)
have (possibly quasi-coherent) deformation theory.

(2)

A lift of
P_{K}:M\longrightarrow{\mathrm{B}}K
to
{\mathrm{B}}{\mathrm{String}}_{\rm CS}(K)
exists if and only if a lift to
{\mathrm{B}}{\mathrm{String}}(K)
exists.

(3)

The
L_{\infty}
-algebra
T\big(*/\,({\operatorname{Hom}}_{v}(M,{\mathrm{B}}{\mathrm{String}}_{\rm CS}(K))/\mathsf{Diff}(M))\big)
has a canonical map to the Lie algebra
TM
, and a
C^{\infty}(M)
-linear splitting of this map (as dg
C^{\infty}(M)
-modules) is the same as a geometric string structure on
P_{K}
together with a symmetric 2-tensor
\gamma\in\Gamma(M,\text{\Large$\vee$}^{2}\,T^{*}M)
.

Compared to Example
6.99
, this allows one to add the data of a principal bundle connection to the
B
-field and Riemannian metric, encoding in this way the bosonic fields of heterotic supergravity; indeed, the modified Bianchi identity for the
H
-flux in this case is captured by
[
92
, Lemma 3.2.4]
. It is also of use in 11-dimensional supergravity, wherein string connections appear as the
{\rm E}_{8}
-model for the 3-form
C
-field of M-theory
[
32
,
35
,
40
]
.

Appendix A
Proof of Theorem
3.2

This appendix is devoted to a proof of Theorem
3.2
.
Let us start by introducing an intermediate
\infty
-category of
C^{\infty}
-geometric formal moduli problems, which sits between
{\mathscr{S}\mathrm{h}}_{\infty}({\mathscr{I}\mathrm{nf}\hskip-0.09995pt\mathscr{M}\mathrm{fd}})
and
L_{\infty}{\mathscr{A}\mathrm{gd}}_{M}
.

Definition A.1

Let
M
be a smooth manifold. Let
{\mathscr{A}\mathrm{rt}}_{/C^{\infty}(M)}
be the smallest full
\infty
-subcategory of
{\mathrm{C}^{\infty}\hskip-0.59998pt\mathscr{R}\mathrm{ing}}^{\infty}_{/C^{\infty}(M)}
satisfying the following two conditions:

(1)

It contains the terminal object
{\mathrm{id}}\colon C^{\infty}(M)\longrightarrow C^{\infty}(M)
.

(2)

It is closed under square-zero extensions by shifted copies of
C^{\infty}(M)
(in non-positive degree). More precisely, for any
n\geqslant 0
and any pullback square in
{\mathrm{C}^{\infty}\hskip-0.59998pt\mathscr{R}\mathrm{ing}}^{\infty}_{/C^{\infty}(M)}
of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{\eta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\infty}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C^{\infty}(M)\oplus C^{\infty}(M)[n+1]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\infty}(M)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces({\mathrm{id}},0)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}

(A.2)

such that
R\longrightarrow C^{\infty}(M)
is in
{\mathscr{A}\mathrm{rt}}_{/C^{\infty}(M)}
, the composition
R_{\eta}\longrightarrow C^{\infty}(M)
is also in
{\mathscr{A}\mathrm{rt}}_{/C^{\infty}(M)}
.

The
\infty
-category of
formal moduli problems
under
M
is the full
\infty
-subcategory

{\mathrm{FMP}}_{M/}\subseteq{\operatorname{Fun}}({\mathscr{A}\mathrm{rt}}_{/C^{\infty}(M)},\mathscr{S})

on the functors
F\colon{\mathscr{A}\mathrm{rt}}_{/C^{\infty}(M)}\longrightarrow\mathscr{S}
with the following properties:

(a)

F\big(C^{\infty}(M)\big)\simeq\ast
.

(b)

F
preserves the pullback squares (
A.2
).

Informally, the definition of a formal moduli problem under a smooth manifold
M
is very similar to that of a prestack with deformation theory (Definition
2.82
), except that a formal moduli problem is only defined on a category of infinitesimal thickenings of
M
and not on all inf-manifolds. In particular, various results and constructions from Section
2.4
have analogues for formal moduli problems.

Example A.3

Every
A\in{\mathscr{A}\mathrm{rt}}_{/C^{\infty}(M)}
determines a
representable
formal moduli problem, which by a slight abuse of notation we write as

{\mathscr{S}\hskip-0.29999pt\mathrm{pf}}(A)\colon{\mathscr{A}\mathrm{rt}}_{/C^{\infty}(M)}\longrightarrow\mathscr{S}\ ,\qquad B\longmapsto{\mathscr{A}\mathrm{rt}}_{/C^{\infty}(M)}\big(A,B\big)\ .

(A.4)

We think of
{\mathscr{S}\hskip-0.29999pt\mathrm{pf}}(A)
as the ‘formal spectrum’ of
A
. For instance, applying this to
C^{\infty}(M)
itself yields the initial formal moduli problem under
M
, which we will simply denote by
M
. Every other formal moduli problem
F
comes with a canonical map
x\colon M\longrightarrow F
, corresponding to the unique point of
F(C^{\infty}(M))
.

Likewise, any filtered colimit of such representable formal moduli problems is again a formal moduli problem; following
[
60
, Definition 1.5.3]
, we will call these
pro-representable
formal moduli problems under
M
.

\triangleleft

Example A.5

Similarly to Example
2.59
, there is an adjoint pair of functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{co}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{pf}}:{\mathrm{cdga}}_{\mathbbm{R}/C^{\infty}(M)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\big({\mathrm{FMP}}_{M/}\big)^{\mathrm{op}}:\Gamma(-,{\mathscr{O}})\ .}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\perp}

The left adjoint sends each
R\longrightarrow C^{\infty}(M)
to the formal moduli problem

{\mathrm{co}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{pf}}(R)\colon{\mathscr{A}\mathrm{rt}}_{/C^{\infty}(M)}\longrightarrow\mathscr{S}\ ,\qquad R^{\prime}\longmapsto{\mathrm{cdga}}_{\mathbbm{R}/C^{\infty}(M)}\big(R,R^{\prime}\big)\ ,

(A.6)

where on the right-hand side we forget from the
C^{\infty}
-ring structure on
R
to its underlying cdga.
On the other hand, it follows that

\Gamma(F,{\mathscr{O}}_{F})\simeq\underset{\alpha}{\operatorname{holim}}\ R_{\alpha}

whenever

F=\underset{\alpha}{\operatorname{hocolim}}\ {\mathscr{S}\hskip-0.29999pt\mathrm{pf}}(R_{\alpha})

is a pro-representable formal moduli problem.

\triangleleft

Let
F
be a formal moduli problem, and let
x\colon M\longrightarrow F
be the map from the initial formal moduli problem. For every
C^{\infty}(M)
-module
I
given as a finite complex of finite free
C^{\infty}(M)
-modules in non-positive degrees, the square-zero extension
C^{\infty}(M)\oplus I
defines an object in
{\mathscr{A}\mathrm{rt}}_{/C^{\infty}(M)}
. By the discussion preceding Definition
2.76
there exists a unique object
T_{x}F
in the
\infty
-category
{\mathscr{M}\mathrm{od}}^{\infty}_{C^{\infty}(M)}
equipped with a natural equivalence

{\mathrm{DK}}\big(T_{x}F\otimes^{h}_{C^{\infty}(M)}I\big)\simeq F(C^{\infty}(M)\oplus I)

for any such
C^{\infty}(M)
-module
I
. When
F=M
is the initial formal moduli problem, this is simply the tangent bundle
TM
. Consequently, every formal moduli problem
F
defines a map of dg
C^{\infty}(M)
-modules
TM\longrightarrow T_{x}F
, and we define

T(M/F)={\mathrm{hofib}}(TM\longrightarrow T_{x}F)

to be its homotopy fibre. By definition this comes endowed with a natural anchor map

T(M/F)\longrightarrow TM.

Theorem A.7 (
[
68
, Theorem 4.2.1 and Remark 4.2.22]
)

There is an equivalence of
\infty
-categories

{\mathrm{FMP}}_{M/}\xrightarrow{\ \simeq\ }L_{\infty}{\mathscr{A}\mathrm{gd}}_{M}^{\infty}\ ,\qquad F\longmapsto T(M/F)\ ,

sending each formal moduli problem under
M
to
T(M/F)
, endowed with a certain
L_{\infty}
-algebroid structure on it.
Furthermore, if
F
is a formal moduli problem under
M
, and
{\mathfrak{g}}
is the associated
L_{\infty}
-algebroid, then there is a natural equivalence

\Gamma(F,{\mathscr{O}}_{F})\simeq{\mathrm{CE}}^{*}({\mathfrak{g}})\ .

In an algebraic setting, this also appears as
[
70
, Theorem 5.1]
; the proof from there carries over verbatum to the
C^{\infty}
-ring setting used here.

We will deduce Theorem
3.2
from Theorem
A.7
by first relating the
\infty
-category of formal moduli problems under
M
to the
\infty
-category of morphisms
x\colon M\longrightarrow X
to a dt prestack
X
, and then passing to associated stacks. For the first step, we consider the canonical fully faithful functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j\colon\big({\mathscr{A}\mathrm{rt}}_{/C^{\infty}(M)}\big)^{\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{PS}\mathrm{h}}_{\infty}({\mathscr{I}\mathrm{nf}\hskip-0.09995pt\mathscr{M}\mathrm{fd}})_{M/}\ ,}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\big(R\longrightarrow C^{\infty}(M)\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\big(M\longrightarrow{\mathscr{S}\hskip-0.29999pt\mathrm{pec}}(R)\big)}

induced by the inclusion
{\mathscr{A}\mathrm{rt}}_{/C^{\infty}(M)}\,\lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow\,{\mathrm{C}^{\infty}\hskip-0.59998pt\mathscr{R}\mathrm{ing}}_{/C^{\infty}(M)}
.
It induces an adjoint pair

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}:{\mathscr{PS}\mathrm{h}}_{\infty}\big({\mathscr{A}\mathrm{rt}}_{/C^{\infty}(M)}^{\mathrm{op}}\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{PS}\mathrm{h}}_{\infty}({\mathscr{I}\mathrm{nf}\hskip-0.09995pt\mathscr{M}\mathrm{fd}})_{M/}:j^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\perp}

(A.8)

where
j_{!}
is given by left Kan extension and
j^{*}
sends every object
x\colon M\longrightarrow X
to the functor

j^{*}X(R)=X({\mathscr{S}\hskip-0.29999pt\mathrm{pec}}(R))\times^{h}_{X(M)}\{x\}\ .

Lemma A.9

Let
M
be a smooth manifold.

(1)

If
x\colon M\longrightarrow X
is a map to a dt prestack, then
j^{*}X
is a formal moduli problem. Furthermore, the tangent complex of
X
at
M
coincides with the tangent complex of the associated formal moduli problem, i.e. there is a natural equivalence of
C^{\infty}(M)
-modules

T_{x}X\simeq T_{x}(j^{*}X)\ .

(2)

Let
x\colon M\longrightarrow X
and
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
be maps of qcdt stacks inducing equivalences
M\simeq X_{\mathrm{red}}\simeq Y_{\mathrm{red}}
. Then
f
is an equivalence if and only if
j^{*}(f)\colon j^{*}X\longrightarrow j^{*}Y
is an equivalence.
In other words, the functors
(-)_{\mathrm{red}}
and
j^{*}
jointly detect equivalences in
\mathrm{St}^{\mathrm{qcdt}}_{M/}
.

Proof.

Assertion (1) follows directly from the formula for
j^{*}
.
Assertion (2) follows from Proposition
2.87
: indeed, every map
x^{\prime}\colon N\longrightarrow X
from a smooth manifold factors over
x\colon M\longrightarrow X
. The conditions on
X
and
Y
then guarantee that the map
T_{x^{\prime}}X\longrightarrow T_{f(x^{\prime})}Y
arises from
T_{x}X\longrightarrow T_{f(x)}Y
by tensoring up and completing (Proposition
2.86
). Since
T_{x}X\longrightarrow T_{f(x)}Y
is an equivalence, the result follows.
∎

We will now study the left adjoint
j_{!}
in more detail.

Proposition A.10

Let
F\colon{\mathscr{A}\mathrm{rt}}_{/C^{\infty}(M)}\longrightarrow\mathscr{S}
be a formal moduli problem under
M
.

(1)

The map
M\longrightarrow j_{!}F
induces an equivalence
M\simeq(j_{!}F)_{\mathrm{red}}
.

(2)

The prestack
j_{!}F
is a
strict
qcdt prestack (see Definition
2.82
).

(3)

The unit map
F\longrightarrow j^{*}\,j_{!}F
is an equivalence.

Proof.

We will prove all assertions in increasing level of generality. Let us start by treating the case where
F
is representable, i.e.
F={\mathscr{S}\hskip-0.29999pt\mathrm{pf}}(A)
for some
A\in{\mathscr{A}\mathrm{rt}}_{/C^{\infty}(M)}
. In this case,
j_{!}F
is the representable sheaf
{\mathscr{S}\hskip-0.29999pt\mathrm{pec}}(A)
(recall from Proposition
2.56
than
{\mathscr{I}\mathrm{nf}\hskip-0.09995pt\mathscr{M}\mathrm{fd}}
is subcanonical), so that
(j_{!}F)_{\mathrm{red}}\simeq M
and
j^{*}\,j_{!}F\simeq F
since
j
is fully faithful. The representable sheaf
j_{!}F={\mathscr{S}\hskip-0.29999pt\mathrm{pec}}(A)
satisfies the Schlessinger condition, as it sends any pushout of inf-manifolds to a pullback.

Using Lemma
2.79
, the strong tangent representability for
j_{!}F
is a consequence of the following observation: for any map
y\colon N\longrightarrow{\mathscr{S}\hskip-0.29999pt\mathrm{pec}}(A)
from a smooth manifold and a bounded complete non-positively graded
C^{\infty}(N)
-module
I
, we need to check that the natural map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{DK}}\big(T_{y}{\mathscr{S}\hskip-0.29999pt\mathrm{pec}}(A)\otimes^{h}_{C^{\infty}(N)}I\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Der}}_{{\mathscr{S}\hskip-0.24582pt\mathrm{pec}}(A)}(y;I)}

(A.11)

is an equivalence. The spaces in (
A.11
) can be identified explicitly in terms of the
C^{\infty}
-algebraic cotangent complex
L_{A}
of the dg
C^{\infty}
-ring
A
, as in Examples
2.77
and
2.89
. Since the canonical point
x\colon M\longrightarrow{\mathscr{S}\hskip-0.29999pt\mathrm{pec}}(A)
induces an isomorphism on reductions, the point
y
factors through
x
as

N\longrightarrow M\longrightarrow{\mathscr{S}\hskip-0.29999pt\mathrm{pec}}(A)\ ,

for some smooth map of manifolds
f\colon N\longrightarrow M
. Using this decomposition, the map (
A.11
) can be identified in a purely algebraic way as the Dold–Kan image of

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Hom}}_{C^{\infty}(M)}\big(L_{A}\otimes_{A}C^{\infty}(M),C^{\infty}(N)\big)\otimes^{h}_{C^{\infty}(N)}I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Hom}}_{C^{\infty}(M)}\big(L_{A}\otimes_{A}C^{\infty}(M),f_{*}I\big)\ .}

(A.12)

For the
canonical
basepoint
x:M\longrightarrow{\mathscr{S}\hskip-0.29999pt\mathrm{pec}}(A)
, there is an explicit model for
L_{A}\otimes_{A}C^{\infty}(M)
. Indeed, by
[
68
, Corollary 4.2.18]
there exists a dg Lie algebroid
{\mathfrak{g}}
whose underlying complex is a projectively cofibrant, negatively graded complex of finite free
C^{\infty}(M)
-modules such that
A\simeq{\mathrm{CE}}^{*}({\mathfrak{g}})
. Using this and
[
68
, Lemmas 4.1.33 and 4.1.35]
, one finds

\displaystyle L_{A}\otimes_{A}C^{\infty}(M)

\displaystyle\cong\Omega^{1}(M)\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}^{\vee}\ ,

\displaystyle{\operatorname{Hom}}_{C^{\infty}(M)}\big(L_{A}\otimes_{A}C^{\infty}(M),C^{\infty}(N)\big)

\displaystyle\cong\big(TM\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}[1]\big)\otimes_{C^{\infty}(M)}C^{\infty}(N)\ ,

with a certain differential, where
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\vee}
denotes the
C^{\infty}(M)
-linear dual complex of
{\mathfrak{g}}
.
This is a non-positively graded complex of finite free
C^{\infty}(M)
-modules and hence cofibrant. It follows that both of these complexes are projectively cofibrant complexes of finite-type projective
C^{\infty}(N)
-modules. If we use these models, then there is no need to derive the tensor product in (
A.12
), and one sees directly that the map in (
A.12
) becomes an isomorphism because
I
is bounded. It follows that
{\mathscr{S}\hskip-0.29999pt\mathrm{pf}}(A)
has strictly quasi-coherent deformation theory.

Next suppose that
F
is a pro-representable formal moduli problem (Example
A.3
). Since both
j_{!}
and
j^{*}
preserve filtered colimits, as does reduction, it follows that
(j_{!}F)_{\mathrm{red}}\simeq M
and the unit map
F\longrightarrow j^{*}\,j_{!}F
is an equivalence. Proposition
2.91
implies that
j_{!}F
has quasi-coherent deformation theory; its proof shows that
\kappa_{!}\,\kappa^{*}{\mathrm{Der}}_{j_{!}F}\longrightarrow{\mathrm{Der}}_{j_{!}F}
is a filtered colimit of equivalences, so that
j_{!}F
satisfies strong tangent representability.

Finally, let
F
be a general formal moduli problem. By
[
60
, Proposition 1.5.8]
, there exists a simplicial diagram of formal moduli problems
F_{\bullet}
such that
|F_{\bullet}|\simeq F
and each
F_{n}
is pro-representable. Moreover, for each horn inclusion the induced map of tangent complexes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{x}(F_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{x}\big(F(\Lambda^{n}_{i})\big)=\underset{\Delta^{k}\longrightarrow\Lambda^{n}_{i}}{\operatorname{holim}}\ T_{x}(F_{k})}

has a fibre whose cohomology groups vanish in positive degrees, because the matching maps are formally smooth and this is detected on tangent complexes (by an argument for formal moduli problems analogous to that of Proposition
2.102
).
This gives rise to a simplicial diagram
j_{!}(F_{\bullet})
of qcdt prestacks (with strong tangent representability) such that
|j_{!}(F_{\bullet})|\simeq j_{!}(F)
since
j_{!}
preserves colimits.

We claim that
j_{!}(F_{\bullet})
is a formally smooth
\infty
-groupoid, that is, for each horn inclusion the induced map

f\colon j_{!}(F_{n})\longrightarrow X\coloneqq\underset{\Delta^{k}\longrightarrow\Lambda^{n}_{i}}{\operatorname{holim}}\ j_{!}(F_{k})

is formally smooth. By Proposition
2.102
, it suffices to verify that, for every point
y\colon N\longrightarrow j_{!}(F_{n})
from a smooth manifold, the fibre of the map
T_{y}\big(j_{!}(F_{n})\big)\longrightarrow T_{f(y)}X
has vanishing cohomology groups in positive degrees. Since the reduction of
j_{!}(F_{n})
is equivalent to
M
(by the second step of this proof), the map
y
again factors over the canonical point
M\longrightarrow j_{!}(F_{n})
. Since both
j_{!}(F_{n})
and
X
are qcdt prestacks with strong tangent representability (by Proposition
2.91
and its proof), their tangent complexes at the point
y
are obtained by pulling back their tangent complexes at the canonical point.

It thus suffices to verify that, for the canonical point
x\colon M\longrightarrow j_{!}(F_{n})
, the map

T_{x}\big(j_{!}(F_{n})\big)\simeq T_{x}\big(j^{*}\,j_{!}(F_{n})\big)\longrightarrow\underset{\Delta^{k}\longrightarrow\Lambda^{n}_{i}}{\operatorname{holim}}\ T_{x}\big(j^{*}\,j_{!}(F_{k})\big)\simeq T_{x}X

has a fibre with vanishing cohomology in positive degrees. Here the last equivalence uses that
j^{*}
preserves homotopy limits. Since
F_{\bullet}\simeq j^{*}\,j_{!}F_{\bullet}
, this claim follows from the construction of
F_{\bullet}
.
We conclude that
j_{!}F_{\bullet}
is a formally smooth
\infty
-groupoid, so that its colimit
j_{!}F
is a qcdt prestack (with strong tangent representability) by Proposition
2.106
. Since reduction preserves colimits, it also follows that
M\simeq(j_{!}F)_{\mathrm{red}}
. To see that the unit map
F\longrightarrow j^{*}\,j_{!}F
is an equivalence of formal moduli problems, it suffices to verify that the induced map on tangent complexes is an equivalence. This follows again from Proposition
2.106
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.2
.

Let us write

\mathrm{St}^{\mathrm{dt}}_{M/}\subseteq\mathrm{PreSt}^{\mathrm{dt}}_{M/}\subseteq{\mathscr{PS}\mathrm{h}}_{\infty}({\mathscr{I}\mathrm{nf}\hskip-0.09995pt\mathscr{M}\mathrm{fd}})_{M/}

for the full
\infty
-subcategories of maps
M\longrightarrow X
where
X
is a stack (resp. prestack) with deformation theory. By Theorem
A.7
and Lemma
A.9
, there are functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(M/-)\colon\mathrm{St}^{\mathrm{dt}}_{M/}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{PreSt}^{\mathrm{dt}}_{M/}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{FMP}}_{M/}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{\infty}{\mathscr{A}\mathrm{gd}}_{M}^{\infty}\ ,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

(A.13)

where
j^{*}
is the restriction along
j
from Diagram (
A.8
). The composition sends each stack
X
with deformation theory to
T(M/X)
equipped with a certain
L_{\infty}
-algebroid structure on it. Proposition
A.10
implies that each of the three functors in (
A.13
) admits a left adjoint, giving

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{\infty}{\mathscr{A}\mathrm{gd}}_{M}^{\infty}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{FMP}}_{M/}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{PreSt}^{\mathrm{dt}}_{M/}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{St}^{\mathrm{dt}}_{M/}\ .}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\longmapsto\widetilde{X}}

(A.14)

Here
j_{!}
is the functor from (
A.8
), i.e. taking left Kan extension along
j
, and the last functor takes associated sheaves. By Proposition
A.10
,
j_{!}(F)
is a qcdt prestack, so taking associated sheaves yields a map

x\colon M\longrightarrow\widetilde{j_{!}(F)}

to a qcdt stack by Proposition
2.91
.

The proof of Proposition
2.84
shows that
T_{x}\widetilde{j_{!}F}
arises as the global sections of the sheaf associated to the functor which sends an open subspace
u\colon U\,\lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow\,M
to

T_{u^{*}x}j_{!}F\simeq T_{x}j_{!}F\otimes_{C^{\infty}(M)}C^{\infty}(U)\ .

(A.15)

Here we used that
j_{!}F
has strictly quasi-coherent deformation theory (see Definition
2.82
and Proposition
A.10
). Consequently

T_{x}\widetilde{j_{!}(F)}\simeq\big(T_{x}j_{!}F\big)^{\mathrm{cpl}}\simeq\big(T_{x}F\big)^{\mathrm{cpl}}

is the completion of the tangent complex of
F
.
Using this, one sees that the unit map
F\longrightarrow j^{*}\widetilde{j_{!}F}
is an equivalence if and only if
T_{x}F
is a complete complex of
C^{\infty}(M)
-modules. Equivalently, this means that the corresponding
L_{\infty}
-algebroid is complete.

We conclude that the restriction of the functor (
A.14
) to the complete
L_{\infty}
-algebroids is fully faithful, with essential image contained in the full
\infty
-subcategory of qcdt stacks
X
under
M
inducing an equivalence
M\simeq X_{\mathrm{red}}
. To show that this is an equivalence, it now remains to verify that the right adjoint (
A.13
) detects equivalences between qcdt stacks under
M
whose reduction is
M
.
This can be seen as follows:
let

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p(x)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

(A.16)

be a morphism of qcdt stacks under
M
.
Suppose also that the maps
x\colon M\longrightarrow Y
and
p(x)\colon M\longrightarrow X
induce equivalences on reductions, i.e.
X_{\mathrm{red}}\simeq M\simeq Y_{\mathrm{red}}
, and that the induced morphism

T(M/X)\longrightarrow T(M/Y)

is an equivalence of
L_{\infty}
-algebroids on
M
.
We will deduce the claim from Proposition
2.87
, by which it would suffice to show that the induced map
T_{y}Y\longrightarrow T_{p(y)}X
is an equivalence in
{\mathscr{M}\mathrm{od}}_{C^{\infty}(M)}^{\infty}
, for every manifold point
y\colon N\longrightarrow Y
.

First we observe that since the canonical morphism
M\longrightarrow Y_{\mathrm{red}}
is an equivalence, any point
y\colon N\longrightarrow Y
factors canonically through the map
x:M\longrightarrow Y
.
By Remark
2.88
, and since
Y
and
X
are qcdt stacks, it thus suffices to show that the morphism
T_{x}Y\longrightarrow T_{p(x)}X
is an equivalence.
We now use that the map
T(M/X)\longrightarrow T(M/Y)
is an equivalence of
L_{\infty}
-algebroids.
This induces, in particular, an equivalence of the underlying dg
C^{\infty}(M)
-modules (which we denote by the same symbols).
Since
T(M/Y)
is the homotopy fibre
{\mathrm{hofib}}(TM\longrightarrow T_{x}Y)
, and analogously
T(M/X)={\mathrm{hofib}}(TM\longrightarrow T_{p(x)}X)
, we recover
T_{x}Y
as the homotopy fibre of the anchor map up to a shift,

T_{x}Y\simeq{\mathrm{hofib}}(T_{x}Y\longrightarrow TM)[1]\ ,

(A.17)

and analogously for
T_{p(x)}X
.
Thus
T_{x}Y\longrightarrow T_{p(x)}X
is an equivalence, and so the claim follows.

Finally, it remains to identify the function algebra on such a qcdt stack
X
under
M
with the Chevalley–Eilenberg complex
{\mathrm{CE}}^{*}(T(M/X))
. To that end, let
F=j^{*}X
be the corresponding formal moduli problem. By
[
60
, Proposition 1.5.8]
, one can write

F=\underset{\alpha}{\operatorname{colim}}\ {\mathscr{S}\hskip-0.29999pt\mathrm{pf}}(A_{\alpha})

as a sifted colimit of a diagram of corepresentable formal moduli problems. Consequently, we can write
X
as a sifted colimit

X\simeq\widetilde{j_{!}F}\simeq\underset{\alpha}{\operatorname{colim}}\ {\mathscr{S}\hskip-0.29999pt\mathrm{pec}}(A_{\alpha})

of representable sheaves in
{\mathscr{S}\mathrm{h}}_{\infty}({\mathscr{I}\mathrm{nf}\hskip-0.09995pt\mathscr{M}\mathrm{fd}})
. Since the function algebras from Examples
2.59
and
A.5
both send colimits to limits of cdgas, we obtain the desired equivalence

{\mathrm{CE}}^{*}\big(T(M/X)\big)\simeq\Gamma(F,{\mathscr{O}}_{F})\simeq\underset{\alpha}{\operatorname{lim}}\ A_{\alpha}\simeq\Gamma(j_{!}F,{\mathscr{O}}_{j_{!}F})\simeq\Gamma(X,{\mathscr{O}}_{X})

where the first equivalence is provided by Theorem
A.7
.
∎

Appendix B
Proof of Theorem
3.14

We will now turn to the proof of Theorem
3.14
. Let us start by giving a more precise definition of
\mathsf{Diff}(M)
as an
\infty
-group in
{\mathscr{S}\mathrm{h}}_{\infty}({\mathscr{I}\mathrm{nf}\hskip-0.09995pt\mathscr{M}\mathrm{fd}})
in terms of its classifying stack.

Definition B.1

Let
M
be a smooth manifold. The stack

{\mathrm{B}}\mathsf{Diff}(M)\colon{\mathscr{I}\mathrm{nf}\hskip-0.09995pt\mathscr{M}\mathrm{fd}}^{\mathrm{op}}\longrightarrow\mathscr{S}

is the functor sending each inf-manifold
U
to the maximal
\infty
-subgroupoid of
{\mathscr{I}\mathrm{nf}\hskip-0.09995pt\mathscr{M}\mathrm{fd}}_{/U}
on those objects
p\colon E\longrightarrow U
such that the homotopy pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E\big|U_{\mathrm{red}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{\mathrm{red}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}

defines a fibre bundle
E\big|U_{\mathrm{red}}\longrightarrow U_{\mathrm{red}}
of (ordinary) smooth manifolds with fibres diffeomorphic to
M
. There is a canonical basepoint
\ast\longrightarrow{\mathrm{B}}\mathsf{Diff}(M)
, classifying the trivial fibre bundle
M\longrightarrow\ast
.

Lemma B.2

(1)

The basepoint
\ast\longrightarrow{\mathrm{B}}\mathsf{Diff}(M)
is an effective epimorphism in
{\mathscr{S}\mathrm{h}}_{\infty}({\mathscr{I}\mathrm{nf}\hskip-0.09995pt\mathscr{M}\mathrm{fd}})
.

(2)

There is an equivalence
{\mathsf{\Omega}}({\mathrm{B}}\mathsf{Diff}(M))\simeq\mathsf{Diff}(M)
of group objects in
{\mathscr{S}\mathrm{h}}_{\infty}({\mathscr{I}\mathrm{nf}\hskip-0.09995pt\mathscr{M}\mathrm{fd}})
.

In particular, this implies that
{\mathrm{B}}\mathsf{Diff}(M)
is indeed the classifying stack of the diffeomorphism group from Definition
3.11
, and that it has deformation theory by Lemma
3.12
.

Proof.

A morphism
f\colon Y\longrightarrow X
in
{\mathscr{S}\mathrm{h}}_{\infty}({\mathscr{I}\mathrm{nf}\hskip-0.09995pt\mathscr{M}\mathrm{fd}})
is an effective epimorphism if and only if the morphism
\pi_{0}f\colon\pi_{0}Y\longrightarrow\pi_{0}X
it induces is an epimorphism
[
58
, Proposition 7.2.1.14]
.
For any
Y\in{\mathscr{S}\mathrm{h}}_{\infty}({\mathscr{I}\mathrm{nf}\hskip-0.09995pt\mathscr{M}\mathrm{fd}})
, the sheaf
\pi_{0}Y
arises as the sheafification of the
\infty
-presheaf
U\longmapsto\pi_{0}(Y(U))
(which is zero-truncated, i.e. a sheaf of sets).
Thus to show that
f
is an effective epimorphism in
{\mathscr{S}\mathrm{h}}_{\infty}({\mathscr{I}\mathrm{nf}\hskip-0.09995pt\mathscr{M}\mathrm{fd}})
, it suffices to show that, for each
U\in{\mathscr{S}\mathrm{h}}_{\infty}({\mathscr{I}\mathrm{nf}\hskip-0.09995pt\mathscr{M}\mathrm{fd}})
, the induced map
\pi_{0}(f_{|U})\colon\pi_{0}(Y(U))\longrightarrow\pi_{0}(X(U))
is surjective.

To show this for the map
*\longrightarrow{\mathrm{B}}\mathsf{Diff}(M)
, suppose that
U
is an inf-manifold and let
p\colon E\longrightarrow U
be classified by a map
U\longrightarrow{\mathrm{B}}\mathsf{Diff}(M)
.
By the above discussion, it suffices show that
p
is locally trivial.
To that end, observe that since
E\times_{U}U_{\mathrm{red}}\longrightarrow U_{\mathrm{red}}
is a fibre bundle, we can find a cover
\{U_{a}\}_{a\in\varLambda}
of
U
such that
E\times_{U}(U_{a})_{\mathrm{red}}\longrightarrow(U_{a})_{\mathrm{red}}
is the trivial bundle for each
a\in\varLambda
. It then suffices to show that
E\times_{U}U_{a}\longrightarrow U_{a}
is trivial as well, for each index
a
.

To see this, consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\times(U_{a})_{\mathrm{red}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E\times_{U}U_{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\times(U_{a})_{\mathrm{red}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(U_{a})_{\mathrm{red}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(U_{a})_{\mathrm{red}}}

in which the left square is a pullback. By Corollary
2.41
, there exists a dashed map
U_{a}\longrightarrow(U_{a})_{\mathrm{red}}
such that the bottom row composes to the identity. There then exists a dashed map
E\times_{U}U_{a}\longrightarrow M\times(U_{a})_{\mathrm{red}}
making the diagram commute, such that the top row composes to the identity: indeed, this follows from the fact that
M\times(U_{a})_{\mathrm{red}}\longrightarrow E\times_{U}U_{a}
is of the form
{\mathscr{S}\hskip-0.29999pt\mathrm{pec}}(A)\longrightarrow{\mathscr{S}\hskip-0.29999pt\mathrm{pec}}(A^{\prime})
where
\mathrm{H}^{0}(A^{\prime})\longrightarrow\mathrm{H}^{0}(A)
is surjective with nilpotent kernel, while the right vertical map is formally smooth since it is a submersion (see Example
2.103
). The right-hand square then induces a map
f\colon E\times_{U}U_{a}\longrightarrow M\times U_{a}
over
U_{a}
, whose base change along
(U_{a})_{\mathrm{red}}\longrightarrow U_{a}
is an equivalence. It follows from Lemma
2.38
(2) that
f
is an equivalence, so that
E\times_{U}U_{a}\longrightarrow U_{a}
is indeed trivial.
This shows that
\ast\longrightarrow{\mathrm{B}}\mathsf{Diff}(M)
is an effective epimorphism.

Unravelling the definitions, the space of maps
U\longrightarrow{\mathsf{\Omega}}({\mathrm{B}}\mathsf{Diff}(M))
is now the union of path components in
{\mathscr{I}\mathrm{nf}\hskip-0.09995pt\mathscr{M}\mathrm{fd}}(U\times M,M)
consisting of
\phi\colon U\times M\longrightarrow M
such that
({\mathrm{id}},\phi)\colon U\times M\longrightarrow U\times M
is an equivalence of inf-manifolds. This is precisely the space of
U
-points of the stack
\mathsf{Diff}(M)
.
∎

Let us now turn to the construction of the right adjoint

{\operatorname{Hom}}^{\wedge}\colon\mathrm{St}^{\mathrm{dt}}_{M/}\longrightarrow\mathrm{St}^{\mathrm{dt}}_{\ast{/\hskip-2.84544pt/}{\mathrm{B}}\mathsf{Diff}(M)}

from Theorem
3.14
. The stack
{\mathrm{B}}\mathsf{Diff}(M)
comes with a universal bundle
p\colon M/\mathsf{Diff}(M)\longrightarrow{\mathrm{B}}\mathsf{Diff}(M)
which fits into a pullback square (see for instance
[
66
,
67
,
20
,
21
]
)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ast}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M/\mathsf{Diff}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{B}}\mathsf{Diff}(M)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

One can also describe
p
as the associated bundle for the canonical action of
\mathsf{Diff}(M)
on
M
.

Since
{\mathscr{S}\mathrm{h}}_{\infty}({\mathscr{I}\mathrm{nf}\hskip-0.09995pt\mathscr{M}\mathrm{fd}})
is locally cartesian closed, we then obtain a composite adjoint pair

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{S}\mathrm{h}}_{\infty}({\mathscr{I}\mathrm{nf}\hskip-0.09995pt\mathscr{M}\mathrm{fd}})_{/{\mathrm{B}}\mathsf{Diff}(M)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{S}\mathrm{h}}_{\infty}({\mathscr{I}\mathrm{nf}\hskip-0.09995pt\mathscr{M}\mathrm{fd}})_{/(M/\mathsf{Diff}(M))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{S}\mathrm{h}}_{\infty}({\mathscr{I}\mathrm{nf}\hskip-0.09995pt\mathscr{M}\mathrm{fd}})\ .}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\perp}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\perp}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t^{*}}

(B.3)

Here the functor
p^{*}
takes the pullback along
p
, and
t_{!}
post-composes with the canonical morphism

t\colon M/\mathsf{Diff}(M)\longrightarrow\ast\ .

The composite right adjoint
p_{*}\,t^{*}
first sends
X
to
X\times M/\mathsf{Diff}(M)
and then takes the internal mapping object relative to
{\mathrm{B}}\mathsf{Diff}(M)
.
We can equivalently describe
p_{*}\,t^{*}(X)\longrightarrow{\mathrm{B}}\mathsf{Diff}(M)
by the object
q^{*}\,p_{*}\,t^{*}(X)
endowed with a
\mathsf{Diff}(M)
-action, obtained by pulling back along the Čech nerve of the map
q
. Since
q^{*}\,p_{*}\simeq p^{\prime}_{*}\,q^{\prime*}
(which holds in any locally cartesian closed
\infty
-category), one sees that
q^{*}\,p_{*}\,t^{*}(X)\simeq{\operatorname{Hom}}(M,X)
, with
\mathsf{Diff}(M)
-action given by pre-composition. In light of this, let us introduce the following notation: we set

{\operatorname{Hom}}(M,X)\,\big/\,\mathsf{Diff}(M)\coloneqq p_{*}\,t^{*}(X)\ \in\ {\mathscr{S}\mathrm{h}}_{\infty}({\mathscr{I}\mathrm{nf}\hskip-0.09995pt\mathscr{M}\mathrm{fd}})_{/{\mathrm{B}}\mathsf{Diff}(M)}\ .

(B.4)

The functor
t_{!}\,p^{*}
sends the canonical basepoint
q\colon\ast\longrightarrow{\mathrm{B}}\mathsf{Diff}(M)
to
M
. Consequently, the adjoint pair (
B.3
) induces an adjoint pair on under-categories

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{!}\,p^{*}:{\mathscr{S}\mathrm{h}}_{\infty}({\mathscr{I}\mathrm{nf}\hskip-0.09995pt\mathscr{M}\mathrm{fd}})_{*{/\hskip-2.84544pt/}{\mathrm{B}}\mathsf{Diff}(M)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{S}\mathrm{h}}_{\infty}({\mathscr{I}\mathrm{nf}\hskip-0.09995pt\mathscr{M}\mathrm{fd}})_{M/}:p_{*}\,t^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\perp}

where
t_{!}\,p^{*}
sends

\ast\longrightarrow Y\longrightarrow{\mathrm{B}}\mathsf{Diff}(M)

to the pullback

M=t_{!}\,p^{*}(\ast)\longrightarrow t_{!}\,p^{*}(Y)=Y\times_{{\mathrm{B}}\mathsf{Diff}(M)}M/\mathsf{Diff}(M)\ .

Its right adjoint acts as

p_{*}\,t^{*}\big(x\colon M\longrightarrow X\big)={\operatorname{Hom}}(M,X)\,\big/\,\mathsf{Diff}(M)

with basepoint

x^{\dashv}\colon\ast\longrightarrow{\operatorname{Hom}}(M,X)\,\big/\,\mathsf{Diff}(M)

induced under the internal hom adjunction by the map
x
.

Lemma B.5

The functors
t_{!}\,p^{*}
and
p_{*}\,t^{*}
both preserve dt stacks.

Proof.

Since
Y
,
{\mathrm{B}}\mathsf{Diff}(M)
and
M/\mathsf{Diff}(M)
are all dt stacks,
t_{!}\,p^{*}(Y)
is a dt stack as well. If
X\in\mathrm{St}^{\mathrm{dt}}_{M/}
, then
{\operatorname{Hom}}(M,X)
is a dt stack by Proposition
2.91
.
It follows that
{\operatorname{Hom}}(M,X)/\mathsf{Diff}(M)
is the quotient of a formally smooth
\infty
-groupoid (Definition
2.104
) and hence a dt stack as well.
∎

Corollary B.6

There is an adjoint pair

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{!}\,p^{*}:\mathrm{St}^{\mathrm{dt}}_{\ast{/\hskip-2.84544pt/}{\mathrm{B}}\mathsf{Diff}(M)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{St}^{\mathrm{dt}}_{M/}:p_{*}\,t^{*}\ .}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\perp}

It now remains to show that the functor
p_{*}\,t^{*}
is compatible with Lie differentiation. Instead of constructing the diagram of right adjoints from Theorem
3.14
, our strategy will be to construct the corresponding diagram of
left
adjoints. The advantage of working with left adjoints is that it will suffice to describe them on generators.

Remark B.7

Let
\mathscr{V}
be a presentable
\infty
-category. A small full
\infty
-subcategory
\mathscr{C}\subseteq\mathscr{V}
is said to be
dense
if the induced colimit-preserving functor
{\mathscr{PS}\mathrm{h}}_{\infty}(\mathscr{C})\longrightarrow\mathscr{V}
is a localisation (equivalently its right adjoint is fully faithful). This implies that for any other presentable
\infty
-category
\mathscr{W}
, restriction to
\mathscr{C}
defines a fully faithful functor
{\operatorname{Fun}}^{{\mathrm{L}}}(\mathscr{V},\mathscr{W})\,\lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow\,{\operatorname{Fun}}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{W})
.

This is compatible with taking over-categories in the following sense: given an adjoint pair of functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F:\mathscr{V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{W}:G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\perp}

and an object
w\in\mathscr{W}
, consider the induced adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F:\mathscr{V}_{/G(w)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{W}_{/w}:G\ .}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\perp}

If
\mathscr{C}\subseteq\mathscr{V}
is dense, then
\mathscr{C}_{/G(w)}\subseteq\mathscr{V}_{/G(w)}
is dense. The functor
F\colon\mathscr{V}_{/G(w)}\longrightarrow\mathscr{W}_{/w}
is then the unique left adjoint extending

\mathscr{C}_{/G(w)}\simeq\mathscr{C}\times_{\mathscr{W}}\mathscr{W}_{/w}\xrightarrow{\ \pi_{2}\ }\mathscr{W}_{/w}\ ,

where
\pi_{2}
denotes the canonical projection functor.

\triangleleft

Proof of Theorem
3.14
.

Throughout this proof, let us abbreviate
R=C^{\infty}(M)
.
We are going to construct a diagram of left adjoints of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{St}^{\mathrm{dt}}_{M/}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{St}^{\mathrm{dt}}_{\ast{/\hskip-2.84544pt/}{\mathrm{B}}\mathsf{Diff}(M)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{St}^{\mathrm{dt}}_{\ast/}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{\infty}{\mathscr{A}\mathrm{gd}}_{M}^{{\mathrm{cpl}},\infty}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\big(L_{\infty}{\mathscr{A}\mathrm{lg}}^{\infty}_{\mathbbm{R}}\big)_{/\Gamma(M,TM)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{\infty}{\mathscr{A}\mathrm{lg}}^{\infty}_{\mathbbm{R}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{!}\,p^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{fgt}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(R\otimes(-))^{{\mathrm{cpl}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{fgt}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{\ast}}

(B.8)

such that the desired square from Theorem
3.14
is obtained from the left square by passing to right adjoints.

We start by discussing the horizontal functors. The two functors on the right forget the maps to
{\mathrm{B}}\mathsf{Diff}(M)
and
TM
, respectively. The functor
t_{!}\,p^{*}
is the left adjoint to the functor
p_{*}\,t^{*}
from Corollary
B.6
. The bottom left horizontal functor is the left adjoint to the forgetful functor, and sends a map of
\mathbbm{R}
-linear
L_{\infty}
-algebras
{\mathfrak{g}}\longrightarrow TM
to the completion of the
L_{\infty}
-algebroid
R\otimes_{\mathbbm{R}}{\mathfrak{g}}
whose brackets are obtained from those of
{\mathfrak{g}}
via the Leibniz rule.

As for the vertical functors, we define
\Phi_{M}
to be the (fully faithful) left adjoint to the functor
T(M/-)\colon\mathrm{St}^{\mathrm{dt}}_{M/}\longrightarrow L_{\infty}{\mathscr{A}\mathrm{gd}}_{M}^{{\mathrm{cpl}},\infty}
from Theorem
3.2
.
Replacing
M
by the point induces an analogous adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{*}:L_{\infty}{\mathscr{A}\mathrm{lg}}^{\infty}_{\mathbbm{R}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{St}^{\mathrm{dt}}_{*/}:T(*/-)\ .}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\perp}

(B.9)

It now remains to construct a lift of the adjoint pair
(\Phi_{*},T(\ast/-))
to an adjunction on over-categories (which we denote by the same symbols)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{*}:(L_{\infty}{\mathscr{A}\mathrm{lg}}^{\infty}_{\mathbbm{R}})_{/\Gamma(M,TM)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{St}^{\mathrm{dt}}_{*{/\hskip-2.84544pt/}{\mathrm{B}}\mathsf{Diff}(M)}:T(*/-)\ ,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\perp}

(B.10)

that makes both squares commute.

To achieve this, we use that the
\infty
-categories of
L_{\infty}
-algebroids and
L_{\infty}
-algebras over
TM
have dense subcategories given by good objects
[
70
, Proposition 5.4]
. Here an
L_{\infty}
-algebroid (resp.
L_{\infty}
-algebra over
TM
) is said to be
good
if it is weakly equivalent to a cofibrant quasi-free
L_{\infty}
-algebroid (resp.
L_{\infty}
-algebra) on a finite number of generators, each in strictly positive degree; such an
L_{\infty}
-algebroid is automatically complete, because its underlying complex is quasi-isomorphic to a complex of finitely generated free
R
-modules (see Remark
2.26
). By
[
68
, Corollary 4.2.18]
, taking the Chevalley–Eilenberg complex defines equivalences

{\mathrm{CE}}^{*}\colon L_{\infty}{\mathscr{A}\mathrm{gd}}_{M}^{{\mathrm{cpl}},\infty,\mathrm{good}}\xrightarrow{\ \simeq\ }{\mathscr{A}\mathrm{rt}}^{\mathrm{op}}_{/R}\qquad\text{and}\qquad{\mathrm{CE}}^{*}\colon L_{\infty}{\mathscr{A}\mathrm{lg}}_{\mathbbm{R}}^{{\mathrm{cpl}},\infty,\mathrm{good}}\xrightarrow{\ \simeq\ }{\mathscr{A}\mathrm{rt}}^{\mathrm{op}}_{/\mathbbm{R}}\ .

Using this and unravelling the proof of Theorem
3.2
, one sees that the restriction

\Phi_{M}\colon L_{\infty}{\mathscr{A}\mathrm{gd}}_{M}^{{\mathrm{cpl}},\infty,\mathrm{good}}\subseteq L_{\infty}{\mathscr{A}\mathrm{gd}}_{M}^{{\mathrm{cpl}},\infty}\longrightarrow\mathrm{St}^{\mathrm{dt}}_{M/}\ ,\qquad{\mathfrak{g}}\longmapsto\big(M\longrightarrow{\mathscr{S}\hskip-0.29999pt\mathrm{pec}}({\mathrm{CE}}^{*}({\mathfrak{g}}))\big)

sends a good
L_{\infty}
-algebroid
{\mathfrak{g}}
to the spectrum of the Chevalley–Eilenberg complex of
{\mathfrak{g}}
. Similarly, for
M=\ast
we find that
\Phi_{*}\colon L_{\infty}{\mathscr{A}\mathrm{lg}}^{\infty,\mathrm{good}}_{\mathbbm{R}}\longrightarrow\mathrm{St}^{\mathrm{dt}}_{\ast/}
sends
{\mathfrak{g}}
to
{\mathscr{S}\hskip-0.29999pt\mathrm{pec}}({\mathrm{CE}}^{*}({\mathfrak{g}}))
.

In light of Remark
B.7
, it now suffices to provide a dashed functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{St}^{\mathrm{dt}}_{M/}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{St}^{\mathrm{dt}}_{\ast{/\hskip-2.84544pt/}{\mathrm{B}}\mathsf{Diff}(M)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{St}^{\mathrm{dt}}_{\ast/}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{\infty}{\mathscr{A}\mathrm{gd}}_{M}^{\infty,\mathrm{good}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\big(L_{\infty}\mathrm{Alg}_{\mathbbm{R}}^{\infty,\mathrm{good}}\big)_{/\Gamma(M,TM)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{\infty}\mathrm{Alg}_{\mathbbm{R}}^{\infty,\mathrm{good}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{!}\,p^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{fgt}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{S}\hskip-0.24582pt\mathrm{pec}}({\mathrm{CE}}^{*})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\otimes(-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{fgt}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{S}\hskip-0.24582pt\mathrm{pec}}({\mathrm{CE}}^{*})}

(B.11)

such that (a) both squares commute, and (b) the right square is a pullback. Indeed, combining property (b) and the second half of Remark
B.7
implies that the functor
\chi
determines an adjunction (
B.10
) which is nothing but the adjunction (
B.9
) lifted to over-categories. Property (a) then implies that the resulting diagram of left adjoints (
B.8
) commutes.

We now define
\chi
to be the functor which sends each good
L_{\infty}
-algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}\longrightarrow TM
to the morphism
{\mathscr{S}\hskip-0.29999pt\mathrm{pec}}({\mathrm{CE}}^{*}({\mathfrak{g}}))\longrightarrow{\mathrm{B}}\mathsf{Diff}(M)
classifying the fibre bundle

{\mathscr{S}\hskip-0.29999pt\mathrm{pec}}\big({\mathrm{CE}}^{*}(R\otimes{\mathfrak{g}})\big)\longrightarrow{\mathscr{S}\hskip-0.29999pt\mathrm{pec}}\big({\mathrm{CE}}^{*}({\mathfrak{g}})\big)\ .

Note that this is indeed classified by a map to
{\mathrm{B}}\mathsf{Diff}(M)
, because the homotopy pullback along

\ast={\mathscr{S}\hskip-0.29999pt\mathrm{pec}}\big({\mathrm{CE}}^{*}({\mathfrak{g}})\big)_{\mathrm{red}}\longrightarrow{\mathscr{S}\hskip-0.29999pt\mathrm{pec}}\big({\mathrm{CE}}^{*}({\mathfrak{g}})\big)

is equivalent to
M
: it can be computed as the spectrum of
\mathbbm{R}\otimes_{{\mathrm{CE}}^{*}({\mathfrak{g}})}^{h}{\mathrm{CE}}^{*}(R\otimes{\mathfrak{g}})
, which is quasi-isomorphic to
R=C^{\infty}(M)
by
[
70
, Corollary 6.5]
.

With this definition of
\chi
, Diagram (
B.11
) commutes because
t_{!}\,p^{*}
sends a map
Y\longrightarrow{\mathrm{B}}\mathsf{Diff}(M)
to the total space of the corresponding bundle, which here coincides with
{\mathscr{S}\hskip-0.29999pt\mathrm{pec}}({\mathrm{CE}}^{*}(R\otimes{\mathfrak{g}}))
. The right square is a pullback square by
[
68
, Theorem 4.4.1 and Proposition 4.4.12]
: this asserts that the
\infty
-category of good
L_{\infty}
-algebras over
TM
is equivalent to the
\infty
-category of good
L_{\infty}
-algebras equipped with an action on
R=C^{\infty}(M)
by derivations. In turn, this is equivalent to the
\infty
-category of good
L_{\infty}
-algebras
{\mathfrak{g}}
together with a map
{\mathrm{CE}}^{*}({\mathfrak{g}})\longrightarrow A
of dg
C^{\infty}
-rings equipped with an equivalence
\mathbbm{R}\otimes_{{\mathrm{CE}}^{*}({\mathfrak{g}})}^{h}A\simeq R
. Taking spectra and using Definition
B.1
, the latter
\infty
-category is precisely the pullback of the right square in (
B.11
).
∎

Appendix C
Proof of Theorem
5.27

Proof of Theorem
5.27
.

To begin with, recall from the discussion in Section
5.2
(see in particular (
5.40
) and (
5.45
)) that a full connection
\pazocal{A}
on the
(n{-}1)
-gerbe presented by the
{\mathrm{U}}(1)
-cocycle
g
is an
n
-tuple

\pazocal{A}=(A^{(1)},\ldots,A^{(n)})\ ,\quad A^{(p)}\in\Omega^{p}({\check{C}}_{n-p}\,\pazocal{U})\ ,

(C.1)

satisfying the identities

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{d}\log(g)-\check{\delta}A^{(1)}&=0\ ,\\[4.0pt]
\mathrm{d}A^{(p-1)}+(-1)^{p}\,\check{\delta}A^{(p)}&=0\ ,\quad p=2,\ldots,n\ .\end{split}

(C.2)

By Remark
2.13
an
\infty
-morphism
\varPhi_{g,\pazocal{A}}
as desired is the same as a morphism

\phi_{g,\pazocal{A}}\colon\overline{{\mathrm{Sym}}}_{C^{\infty}(M)}\big({\mathrm{At}}_{n-1}(H)[1]\big)\longrightarrow{\check{C}\mathrm{At}}(g)[1]

(C.3)

of graded
C^{\infty}(M)
-modules such that

(1)

its underlying
\mathbbm{R}
-linear map is a Maurer–Cartan element in

{\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathbbm{R}}\Big(\overline{{\mathrm{Sym}}}_{\mathbbm{R}}\big({\mathrm{At}}_{n-1}(H)[1]\big),\,{\check{C}\mathrm{At}}(g)[1]\Big)\ ,

(C.4)

and

(2)

the composition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{{\mathrm{Sym}}}_{C^{\infty}(M)}\big({\mathrm{At}}_{n-1}(H)[1]\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\check{C}\mathrm{At}}(g)[1]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{g,\pazocal{A}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{{\check{C}\mathrm{At}}(g)}[1]}

(C.5)

vanishes on all higher tensor powers of
{\mathrm{At}}_{n-1}(H)[1]
, and its restriction to
{\mathrm{At}}_{n-1}(H)[1]
coincides with the shifted anchor map
\rho_{{\mathrm{At}}_{n-1}(H)}[1]
.

We begin by specifying the morphism
\phi_{g,\pazocal{A}}
.
Note that the shifted complex
{\mathrm{At}}_{n-1}(H)[1]
exhibits vector fields
X\in TM
in degree
-1
and functions
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
in degree
-n
.
We can depict the data of the cochain complexes
\overline{{\mathrm{Sym}}}_{\mathbbm{R}}({\mathrm{At}}_{n-1}(H)[1])
and
{\check{C}\mathrm{At}}(g)[1]
, as well as the components of the morphism
\phi_{g,\pazocal{A}}
, as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{n-1}(g)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{\Large$\wedge$}_{\mathbbm{R}}^{2}TM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\infty}({\check{C}}_{n-2}\,\pazocal{U})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{\Large$\wedge$}_{\mathbbm{R}}^{3}TM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\infty}({\check{C}}_{n-3}\,\pazocal{U})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{\Large$\wedge$}_{\mathbbm{R}}^{n-1}TM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\infty}({\check{C}}_{1}\,\pazocal{U})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{\Large$\wedge$}_{\mathbbm{R}}^{n}TM\oplus C^{\infty}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\infty}({\check{C}}_{0}\,\pazocal{U})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{\Large$\wedge$}_{\mathbbm{R}}^{n+1}TM\oplus\big(TM\otimes_{\mathbbm{R}}C^{\infty}(M)\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces({\mathrm{id}}_{TM},A^{(1)})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{(2)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{\mathrm{CE}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0,(-1)^{2}\,\check{\delta})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{(3)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{\mathrm{CE}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-1)^{3}\,\check{\delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{\mathrm{CE}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-1)^{4}\,\check{\delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{(n-1)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{\mathrm{CE}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-1)^{n-1}\,\check{\delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{(n)}-\check{\delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{\mathrm{CE}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-1)^{n}\,\check{\delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{\mathrm{CE}}}

(C.6)

Here
\delta_{\rm CE}
is the Chevalley–Eilenberg differential on
\overline{{\mathrm{Sym}}}_{\mathbbm{R}}({\mathrm{At}}_{n-1}(H)[1])
given by

\displaystyle\delta_{\rm CE}(v_{1}\cdots v_{k})=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\ \sum_{\sigma\in{\rm Sh}(j,k-j)}\,\epsilon(\sigma)\,[v_{\sigma(1)},\dots,v_{\sigma(j)}]_{{\mathrm{At}}_{n-1}(H),j}\,v_{\sigma(j+1)}\cdots v_{\sigma(k)}

(C.7)

for
v_{j}=(f_{j},X_{j})
with
f_{j}\in C^{\infty}(M)
and
X_{j}\in TM
, where
\mathrm{Sh}(j,k-j)
is the set of
(j,k{-}j)
-shuffle permutations. By Corollary
5.13
, the only non-zero summands are with
j=2
and
j=n+1
.

The squares in Diagram (
C.6
) do
not
commute;
\phi_{g,\pazocal{A}}
is merely a map of graded
C^{\infty}(M)
-modules, not a morphism of chain complexes.
We readily observe that it is compatible with the anchor maps as desired and vanishes on all higher tensor powers of
{\mathrm{At}}_{n-1}(H)[1]
.
The top non-zero row of Diagram (
C.6
) is situated in degree
-1
.
The top horizontal map is well-defined by (
C.2
).

We are thus left to show that

\phi_{g,\pazocal{A}}:\overline{{\mathrm{Sym}}}_{\mathbbm{R}}\big({\mathrm{At}}_{n-1}(H)[1]\big)\longrightarrow{\check{C}\mathrm{At}}[1]

satisfies the Maurer–Cartan equation

\mathrm{d}_{\operatorname{Hom}}\phi_{g,\pazocal{A}}+\sum_{l\geqslant 2}\,\frac{1}{l!}\,[\phi_{g,\pazocal{A}},\dots,\phi_{g,\pazocal{A}}]_{{\operatorname{Hom}},l}=0\ ,

(C.8)

where we use the notation

[\phi_{g,\pazocal{A}},\dots,\phi_{g,\pazocal{A}}]_{{\operatorname{Hom}},l}=[-,\dots,-]_{{\check{C}\mathrm{At}}(g),l}\circ(\phi_{g,\pazocal{A}}\otimes_{\mathbbm{R}}\cdots\otimes_{\mathbbm{R}}\phi_{g,\pazocal{A}})\circ{\mathsf{\Delta}}^{l-1}\ .

(C.9)

Since
{\check{C}\mathrm{At}}(g)
is even a dg Lie algebroid, this bracket is zero unless
l=2
, while
{\mathsf{\Delta}}
is the coproduct on
\overline{{\mathrm{Sym}}}_{\mathbbm{R}}({\mathrm{At}}_{n-1}(H)[1])
with

\displaystyle{\mathsf{\Delta}}(v_{1}\cdots v_{k})=\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\ \sum_{\sigma\in{\rm Sh}(j,k-j)}\,\epsilon(\sigma)\,(v_{\sigma(1)}\cdots v_{\sigma(j)})\otimes(v_{\sigma(j+1)}\cdots v_{\sigma(k)})

(C.10)

and
{\mathsf{\Delta}}^{k}(v_{1}\cdots v_{k})=0
. We now check (
C.8
) in each degree
-k
for
k\geqslant 2
, grouped into cases as follows.

k=2
. Let
X_{1}\wedge X_{2}\in\text{\Large$\wedge$}_{\mathbbm{R}}^{2}TM
.
Then

\displaystyle(\mathrm{d}_{\operatorname{Hom}}\phi_{g,\pazocal{A}})(X_{1}\wedge X_{2})

\displaystyle=(0,(-1)^{2}\,\check{\delta})\circ A^{(2)}(X_{1},X_{2})-({\mathrm{id}}_{TM},A^{(1)})\circ\delta_{\mathrm{CE}}(X_{1}\wedge X_{2})

(C.11)

\displaystyle=\big(-[X_{1},X_{2}],(-1)^{2}\,\check{\delta}A^{(2)}(X_{1},X_{2})-A^{(1)}([X_{1},X_{2}])\big)\ ,

(C.12)

and

\displaystyle[\phi_{g,\pazocal{A}},\phi_{g,\pazocal{A}}]_{{\operatorname{Hom}},2}(X_{1}\wedge X_{2})

(C.13)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt=[-]_{{\check{C}\mathrm{At}}(g),2}\circ(\phi_{g,\pazocal{A}}\otimes\phi_{g,\pazocal{A}})(X_{1}\otimes X_{2}-X_{2}\otimes X_{1})

(C.14)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt=[-]_{{\check{C}\mathrm{At}}(g),2}\big((X_{1},A^{(1)}(X_{1}))\otimes(X_{2},A^{(1)}(X_{2}))-(X_{2},A^{(1)}(X_{2}))\otimes(X_{1},A^{(1)}(X_{1}))\big)

(C.15)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt=2\,\big([X_{1},X_{2}],\pounds_{X_{1}}(A^{(1)}(X_{2}))-\pounds_{X_{2}}(A^{(1)}(X_{1}))\big)\ .

(C.16)

Therefore in total we obtain

\displaystyle\Big(\mathrm{d}_{\operatorname{Hom}}\phi_{g,\pazocal{A}}+\sum_{l\geqslant 2}\,\frac{1}{l!}\,[\phi_{g,\pazocal{A}},\dots,\phi_{g,\pazocal{A}}]_{{\operatorname{Hom}},l}\Big)(X_{1}\wedge X_{2})

(C.17)

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt=\big(-[X_{1},X_{2}],(-1)^{2}\,\check{\delta}A^{(2)}(X_{1},X_{2})-A^{(1)}([X_{1},X_{2}])\big)

(C.18)

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt\qquad\,+\big([X_{1},X_{2}],\pounds_{X_{1}}(A^{(1)}(X_{2}))-\pounds_{X_{2}}(A^{(1)}(X_{1}))\big)

(C.19)

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt=\big(0,((-1)^{2}\,\check{\delta}A^{(2)}+\mathrm{d}A^{(1)})(X_{1},X_{2})\big)

(C.20)

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt=0\ .

(C.21)

3\leqslant k\leqslant n-1
. Let
X_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge X_{k}\in\text{\Large$\wedge$}_{\mathbbm{R}}^{k}TM
.
Note that

\delta_{{\mathrm{CE}}}(X_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge X_{k})=\sum_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant k}\,(-1)^{i+j-1}\,[X_{i},X_{j}]\wedge X_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\widehat{X_{i}}\wedge\cdots\wedge\widehat{X_{j}}\wedge\cdots\wedge X_{k}

(C.22)

(with the
-1
in the exponent guaranteeing that
\delta_{{\mathrm{CE}}}(X_{1}\wedge X_{2})=[X_{1},X_{2}]
). The hat denotes omission of a tensor factor. Then

\displaystyle(\mathrm{d}_{\operatorname{Hom}}\phi_{g,\pazocal{A}})(X_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge X_{k})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k}\,\check{\delta}\big(A^{(k)}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{k})\big)-A^{(k-1)}\circ\delta_{\mathrm{CE}}(X_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge X_{k})

(C.23)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k}\,(\check{\delta}A^{(k)})(X_{1},\ldots,X_{k})

(C.24)

\displaystyle\qquad-\sum_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant k}\,(-1)^{i+j-1}\,A^{(k-1)}\big([X_{i},X_{j}],X_{1},\ldots,\widehat{X_{i}},\ldots,\widehat{X_{j}},\ldots,X_{k}\big)\ .

(C.25)

Moreover, we compute

\displaystyle[\phi_{g,\pazocal{A}},\phi_{g,\pazocal{A}}]_{{\operatorname{Hom}},2}(X_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge X_{k})

(C.26)

\displaystyle\quad=[-]_{{\check{C}\mathrm{At}}(g),2}\circ(\phi_{g,\pazocal{A}}\otimes\phi_{g,\pazocal{A}})\bigg(\sum_{l=1}^{k}\ \sum_{\sigma\in\mathrm{Sh}(l,k-l)}\,\epsilon(\sigma)\,(X_{\sigma(1)}\wedge\cdots\wedge X_{\sigma(l)})\otimes(X_{\sigma(l+1)}\wedge\cdots\wedge X_{\sigma(k)})\bigg)

(C.27)

\displaystyle\quad=[-]_{{\check{C}\mathrm{At}}(g),2}\circ(\phi_{g,\pazocal{A}}\otimes\phi_{g,\pazocal{A}})\bigg(\sum_{\sigma\in\mathrm{Sh}(1,k-1)}\,\epsilon(\sigma)\,X_{\sigma(1)}\otimes(X_{\sigma(2)}\wedge\cdots\wedge X_{\sigma(k)})

(C.28)

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt+\sum_{\sigma\in\mathrm{Sh}(k-1,1)}\,\epsilon(\sigma)\,(X_{\sigma(1)}\wedge\cdots\wedge X_{\sigma(k-1)})\otimes X_{\sigma(k)}\bigg)

(C.29)

\displaystyle\quad=[-]_{{\check{C}\mathrm{At}}(g),2}\bigg(\sum_{\sigma\in\mathrm{Sh}(1,k-1)}\,\epsilon(\sigma)\,\big(X_{\sigma(1)},A^{(1)}(X_{\sigma(1)})\big)\otimes A^{(k-1)}(X_{\sigma(2)}\wedge\cdots\wedge X_{\sigma(k)})

(C.30)

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt+\sum_{\sigma\in\mathrm{Sh}(1,k-1)}\,\epsilon(\sigma)\,A^{(k-1)}(X_{\sigma(1)}\wedge\cdots\wedge X_{\sigma(k-1)})\otimes\big(X_{\sigma(k)},A^{(1)}(X_{\sigma(k)})\big)\bigg)

(C.31)

\displaystyle\quad=2\,\sum_{\sigma\in\mathrm{Sh}(1,k-1)}\,\epsilon(\sigma)\,\pounds_{X_{\sigma(1)}}\big(A^{(k-1)}(X_{\sigma(2)}\wedge\cdots\wedge X_{\sigma(k)})\big)

(C.32)

\displaystyle\quad=2\,\sum_{i=1}^{k}\,(-1)^{i-1}\,\pounds_{X_{i}}\big(A^{(k-1)}(X_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\widehat{X_{i}}\wedge\cdots\wedge X_{k})\big)\ .

(C.33)

Here we have used that
[-]_{{\check{C}\mathrm{At}}(g),2}
gives zero unless at least one of its arguments is in degree one (this allows us to discard all other splittings of the exterior product
X_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge X_{k}
).
Subsequently, we have used the graded antisymmetry of the binary bracket to combine the two sums into one with a factor of
2
.

Combining these results, we obtain

\displaystyle\Big(\mathrm{d}_{\operatorname{Hom}}\phi_{g,\pazocal{A}}+\sum_{l\geqslant 2}\,\frac{1}{l!}\,[\phi_{g,\pazocal{A}},\dots,\phi_{g,\pazocal{A}}]_{{\operatorname{Hom}},l}\Big)(X_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge X_{k})

(C.34)

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt=(-1)^{k}\,(\check{\delta}A^{(k)})(X_{1},\ldots,X_{k})

(C.35)

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt\qquad-\sum_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant k}\,(-1)^{i+j-1}\,A^{(k-1)}\big([X_{i},X_{j}],X_{1},\ldots,\widehat{X_{i}},\ldots,\widehat{X_{j}},\ldots,X_{k}\big)

(C.36)

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt\qquad+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\,(-1)^{i-1}\,\pounds_{X_{i}}\big(A^{(k-1)}(X_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\widehat{X_{i}}\wedge\cdots\wedge X_{k})\big)

(C.37)

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt=\big((-1)^{k}\,\check{\delta}A^{(k)}+\mathrm{d}A^{(k-1)}\big)(X_{1},\ldots,X_{k})

(C.38)

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt=0\ ,

(C.39)

where we have used the global (or coordinate-free) formula for the de Rham differential (see for instance
[
55
, Proposition 14.32]
).

k=n
. We have to evaluate the
\mathbbm{R}
-linear map

\mathrm{d}_{\operatorname{Hom}}\phi_{g,\pazocal{A}}+\sum_{l\geqslant 2}\,\frac{1}{l!}\,[\phi_{g,\pazocal{A}},\dots,\phi_{g,\pazocal{A}}]_{{\operatorname{Hom}},l}\ \in\ {\operatorname{Hom}}_{\mathbbm{R}}\Big(\overline{{\mathrm{Sym}}}_{\mathbbm{R}}\big({\mathrm{At}}_{n-1}(H)[1]),{\check{C}\mathrm{At}}(g)\Big)

(C.40)

on elements of the form
X_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge X_{n}+f
, where
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\in TM
and
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
.
By linearity, we can split this into two computations.
For arguments of the form
X_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge X_{n}\in\text{\Large$\wedge$}_{\mathbbm{R}}^{n}TM
, the computation is exactly as in the case
3\leqslant k\leqslant n-1
above; that is, we obtain

\displaystyle\Big(\mathrm{d}_{\operatorname{Hom}}\phi_{g,\pazocal{A}}+\sum_{l\geqslant 2}\,\frac{1}{l!}\,[\phi_{g,\pazocal{A}},\dots,\phi_{g,\pazocal{A}}]_{{\operatorname{Hom}},l}\Big)(X_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge X_{n})

\displaystyle=\big((-1)^{n}\,\check{\delta}A^{(n)}+\mathrm{d}A^{(n-1)}\big)(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})

(C.41)

\displaystyle=0\ .

(C.42)

It remains to consider arguments of the form
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
.
The Chevalley–Eilenberg differential on
{\mathrm{CE}}_{*}({\mathrm{At}}_{n-1}(H))
satisfies
\delta_{\mathrm{CE}}(f)=0
(see Corollary
5.13
).
Since
\check{\delta}^{2}=0
and also
{\mathsf{\Delta}}(f)=0
, it follows that

\Big(\mathrm{d}_{\operatorname{Hom}}\phi_{g,\pazocal{A}}+\sum_{l\geqslant 2}\,\frac{1}{l!}\,[\phi_{g,\pazocal{A}},\dots,\phi_{g,\pazocal{A}}]_{{\operatorname{Hom}},l}\Big)(f)=0\ .

(C.43)

k=n+1
. We again use linearity to split the computation into two cases.
First, we evaluate

\displaystyle\Big(\mathrm{d}_{\operatorname{Hom}}\phi_{g,\pazocal{A}}+\sum_{l\geqslant 2}\,\frac{1}{l!}\,[\phi_{g,\pazocal{A}},\dots,\phi_{g,\pazocal{A}}]_{{\operatorname{Hom}},l}\Big)(X_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge X_{n+1})\ .

(C.44)

We obtain

\displaystyle(\mathrm{d}_{\operatorname{Hom}}\phi_{g,\pazocal{A}})(X_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge X_{n+1})

(C.45)

\displaystyle\qquad=-\sum_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n+1}\,(-1)^{i+j-1}\,A^{(n)}\big([X_{i},X_{j}],X_{1},\ldots,\widehat{X_{i}},\ldots,\widehat{X_{j}},\ldots,X_{n+1}\big)+(\check{\delta}H)(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n+1})\ ,

(C.46)

and, as in the case
3\leqslant k\leqslant n-1
above,
